Hi. Thank you. Um, I came in today because I'm having a really hard time stopping myself from worrying. It feels like once a worry starts—whether it's about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or just worrying that I missed something at work—I just can't seem to shelve it in my mind.

I try to redirect myself.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: Most nights I lie there replaying the day and planning for every possible problem, then if I wake to hear my mother moving around I’m up for an hour or two afterward and arrive at the library feeling like I never manifestation: Over this past year my worrying has spread from just my mother’s safety to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter, and even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\n- key: S059\n  name: Concentration_Difficulty\n  description: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.\n  manifestation: During cataloging reviews or staff meetings, I’ll be looking right at the screen or the agenda and suddenly realize I haven’t absorbed a word because my mind has wandered into some problem I think I should have I really can't seem to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do something specific to try to make these worries go away?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you. Um, I came in today because I'm having a really hard time stopping myself from worrying. It feels like once a worry starts—whether it's about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or just worrying that I missed something at work—I just can't seem to shelve it in my mind. \n\nI try to. I make lists, and I try really hard to redirect myself to something else, but no matter what I do, I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That's pretty much what I was just trying to describe. Once one of those thoughts starts—like about my mom, or that grant report—it just loops. I try to ignore it, and I try to focus on something else or make my lists, but the thoughts just keep repeating in my head anyway. I really can't seem to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do something specific to try to make these worries go away?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I make those lists I mentioned, but that's just me trying to stay organized and get the tasks out of my head. It's not like I have a specific routine or... or some action I feel forced to do to cancel the worry out. I mostly just try to distract myself, but there's no specific thing I do that actually makes them go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still go to work, I still help my mom... I mean, I might not want to do certain things because I'm tired from worrying so much, but I don't actively avoid places or situations just because of the anxiety. I just push through it, even though it's always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or a racing heart when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my heart doesn't race and I don't get sweaty or shaky or anything like that. I just feel tense and exhausted, but none of those physical things you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or traumatic happened to you recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um, well, not traumatic, but definitely stressful. My mother had a fall, and she ended up having to move into my home so I could help take care of her. That was about a year and two months ago... and honestly, that's exactly when all this started going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you must think certain thoughts or repeat them to yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I just... I worry about things a lot, but I don't feel like I have to repeat certain thoughts or anything. It's just the constant worrying.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one thing, like your mom's health, or is it spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely spread out. At first, it really was just about my mother's safety when she moved in. But over this past year, it's spread to a lot of different things. I worry about our finances, the house, and work—like staff decisions and deadlines. I even stress over whether I’m being a good daughter to her. It just feels like... well, even small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel on me.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has, especially at work. Um, lately I’ve started missing small but important details at the library. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation because I just... I just froze up. That is really not like me at all. It’s honestly made me worry a lot about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you to avoid making decisions or taking on new responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm actively avoiding new responsibilities, no.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Thank you. Um, I came in today because I'm having a really hard time stopping myself from worrying. It feels like once a worry starts—whether it's about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report, or just worrying that I missed something at work—I just can't seem to shelve it in my mind. \n\nI try to. I make lists, and I try really hard to redirect myself to something else, but no matter what I do, I just can't seem to turn it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That's pretty much what I was just trying to describe. Once one of those thoughts starts—like about my mom, or that grant report—it just loops. I try to ignore it, and I try to focus on something else or make my lists, but the thoughts just keep repeating in my head anyway. I really can't seem to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do something specific to try to make these worries go away?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, I make those lists I mentioned, but that's just me trying to stay organized and get the tasks out of my head. It's not like I have a specific routine or... or some action I feel forced to do to cancel the worry out. I mostly just try to distract myself, but there's no specific thing I do that actually makes them go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still go to work, I still help my mom... I mean, I might not want to do certain things because I'm tired from worrying so much, but I don't actively avoid places or situations just because of the anxiety. I just push through it, even though it's always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or a racing heart when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my heart doesn't race and I don't get sweaty or shaky or anything like that. I just feel tense and exhausted, but none of those physical things you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or traumatic happened to you recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um, well, not traumatic, but definitely stressful. My mother had a fall, and she ended up having to move into my home so I could help take care of her. That was about a year and two months ago... and honestly, that's exactly when all this started going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you must think certain thoughts or repeat them to yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I just... I worry about things a lot, but I don't feel like I have to repeat certain thoughts or anything. It's just the constant worrying.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one thing, like your mom's health, or is it spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely spread out. At first, it really was just about my mother's safety when she moved in. But over this past year, it's spread to a lot of different things. I worry about our finances, the house, and work—like staff decisions and deadlines. I even stress over whether I’m being a good daughter to her. It just feels like... well, even small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel on me.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has, especially at work. Um, lately I’ve started missing small but important details at the library. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation because I just... I just froze up. That is really not like me at all. It’s honestly made me worry a lot about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you to avoid making decisions or taking on new responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm actively avoiding new responsibilities, no. I mean, I still do my job and try to handle what comes my way. It's just that making decisions feels a lot harder now because I worry so much about making the wrong one. I haven't stepped down from anything or refused new tasks, it just... takes a lot more out of me to get through them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for about a year and two months now. It all really started right after my mother had a fall and had to move into my home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is worse than would be expected for the situations you're in?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. I mean, at first it made sense to be concerned, but over this past year, the worrying has just... spread. It went from just being about my mother’s safety to constantly worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and... and whether I’m being a good daughter. It's like even the really small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. So, yeah, it definitely feels like it's a lot more than what would be expected for the situations I'm actually in.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider your mother's fall and subsequent move into your home a traumatic event for you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I would call it traumatic, no. It was definitely stressful, and it changed my daily routine a lot, but... I wouldn't use the word trauma. It was just a really difficult adjustment that seemed to trigger all this stress.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so, no. I mean, I haven't noticed any major issues. I probably see my friends a bit less these days just because my hands are full taking care of my mother, but... the worrying itself hasn't really caused problems or arguments with anyone. I mostly just keep all that stress to myself, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. I'm usually up for another hour or two just worrying. I mean, my heart doesn't race and I don't get sweaty or shaky or anything like that. I just feel tense and exhausted, but none of those physical things you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or traumatic happened to you recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um, well, not traumatic, but definitely stressful. My mother had a fall, and she ended up having to move into my home so I could help take care of her. That was about a year and two months ago... and honestly, that's exactly when all this started going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you must think certain thoughts or repeat them to yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I just... I worry about things a lot, but I don't feel like I have to repeat certain thoughts or anything. It's just the constant worrying.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one thing, like your mom's health, or is it spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely spread out. At first, it really was just about my mother's safety when she moved in. But over this past year, it's spread to a lot of different things. I worry about our finances, the house, and work—like staff decisions and deadlines. I even stress over whether I’m being a good daughter to her. It just feels like... well, even small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel on me.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has, especially at work. Um, lately I’ve started missing small but important details at the library. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation because I just... I just froze up. That is really not like me at all. It’s honestly made me worry a lot about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you to avoid making decisions or taking on new responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm actively avoiding new responsibilities, no. I mean, I still do my job and try to handle what comes my way. It's just that making decisions feels a lot harder now because I worry so much about making the wrong one. I haven't stepped down from anything or refused new tasks, it just... takes a lot more out of me to get through them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for about a year and two months now. It all really started right after my mother had a fall and had to move into my home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is worse than would be expected for the situations you're in?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. I mean, at first it made sense to be concerned, but over this past year, the worrying has just... spread. It went from just being about my mother’s safety to constantly worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and... and whether I’m being a good daughter. It's like even the really small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. So, yeah, it definitely feels like it's a lot more than what would be expected for the situations I'm actually in.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider your mother's fall and subsequent move into your home a traumatic event for you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I would call it traumatic, no. It was definitely stressful, and it changed my daily routine a lot, but... I wouldn't use the word trauma. It was just a really difficult adjustment that seemed to trigger all this stress.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so, no. I mean, I haven't noticed any major issues. I probably see my friends a bit less these days just because my hands are full taking care of my mother, but... the worrying itself hasn't really caused problems or arguments with anyone. I mostly just keep all that stress to myself, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. I'm usually up for another hour or two just worrying. By the time I get to work at the library the next morning, I feel like I haven't really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or activities with friends because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not because of the worrying. Like I mentioned earlier, I am seeing people a bit less right now, but that's really just because I'm so busy taking care of my mother. When I actually have a free afternoon, I still want to see my friends and do things. I'm not avoiding anyone... I just don't have the free time I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mostly just try to distract myself, but there's no specific thing I do that actually makes them go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I still go to work, I still help my mom... I mean, I might not want to do certain things because I'm tired from worrying so much, but I don't actively avoid places or situations just because of the anxiety. I just push through it, even though it's always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or a racing heart when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my heart doesn't race and I don't get sweaty or shaky or anything like that. I just feel tense and exhausted, but none of those physical things you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: Has something very stressful or traumatic happened to you recently or in the past?\n\nPatient: Um, well, not traumatic, but definitely stressful. My mother had a fall, and she ended up having to move into my home so I could help take care of her. That was about a year and two months ago... and honestly, that's exactly when all this started going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you must think certain thoughts or repeat them to yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do anything like that. I just... I worry about things a lot, but I don't feel like I have to repeat certain thoughts or anything. It's just the constant worrying.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying mostly about one thing, like your mom's health, or is it spread across many different things?\n\nPatient: It's definitely spread out. At first, it really was just about my mother's safety when she moved in. But over this past year, it's spread to a lot of different things. I worry about our finances, the house, and work—like staff decisions and deadlines. I even stress over whether I’m being a good daughter to her. It just feels like... well, even small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel on me.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it really has, especially at work. Um, lately I’ve started missing small but important details at the library. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation because I just... I just froze up. That is really not like me at all. It’s honestly made me worry a lot about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you to avoid making decisions or taking on new responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm actively avoiding new responsibilities, no. I mean, I still do my job and try to handle what comes my way. It's just that making decisions feels a lot harder now because I worry so much about making the wrong one. I haven't stepped down from anything or refused new tasks, it just... takes a lot more out of me to get through them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for about a year and two months now. It all really started right after my mother had a fall and had to move into my home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is worse than would be expected for the situations you're in?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. I mean, at first it made sense to be concerned, but over this past year, the worrying has just... spread. It went from just being about my mother’s safety to constantly worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and... and whether I’m being a good daughter. It's like even the really small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. So, yeah, it definitely feels like it's a lot more than what would be expected for the situations I'm actually in.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider your mother's fall and subsequent move into your home a traumatic event for you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I would call it traumatic, no. It was definitely stressful, and it changed my daily routine a lot, but... I wouldn't use the word trauma. It was just a really difficult adjustment that seemed to trigger all this stress.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so, no. I mean, I haven't noticed any major issues. I probably see my friends a bit less these days just because my hands are full taking care of my mother, but... the worrying itself hasn't really caused problems or arguments with anyone. I mostly just keep all that stress to myself, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. I'm usually up for another hour or two just worrying. By the time I get to work at the library the next morning, I feel like I haven't really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or activities with friends because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not because of the worrying. Like I mentioned earlier, I am seeing people a bit less right now, but that's really just because I'm so busy taking care of my mother. When I actually have a free afternoon, I still want to see my friends and do things. I'm not avoiding anyone... I just don't have the free time I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or interest in activities you used to enjoy since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My appetite is pretty much the same as always. And I still enjoy doing things I've always liked... like reading or gardening... I just don't have as much time for them right now with my mother. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't felt sad or depressed. I mean, I get a little frustrated sometimes because I'm so tired from not sleeping well, but I wouldn't call it sadness. It's really just the constant worrying that's the issue for me. I don't feel down or hopeless or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean, I still do my job and try to handle what comes my way. It's just that making decisions feels a lot harder now because I worry so much about making the wrong one. I haven't stepped down from anything or refused new tasks, it just... takes a lot more out of me to get through them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for about a year and two months now. It all really started right after my mother had a fall and had to move into my home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is worse than would be expected for the situations you're in?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. I mean, at first it made sense to be concerned, but over this past year, the worrying has just... spread. It went from just being about my mother’s safety to constantly worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and... and whether I’m being a good daughter. It's like even the really small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. So, yeah, it definitely feels like it's a lot more than what would be expected for the situations I'm actually in.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider your mother's fall and subsequent move into your home a traumatic event for you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I would call it traumatic, no. It was definitely stressful, and it changed my daily routine a lot, but... I wouldn't use the word trauma. It was just a really difficult adjustment that seemed to trigger all this stress.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so, no. I mean, I haven't noticed any major issues. I probably see my friends a bit less these days just because my hands are full taking care of my mother, but... the worrying itself hasn't really caused problems or arguments with anyone. I mostly just keep all that stress to myself, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. I'm usually up for another hour or two just worrying. By the time I get to work at the library the next morning, I feel like I haven't really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or activities with friends because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not because of the worrying. Like I mentioned earlier, I am seeing people a bit less right now, but that's really just because I'm so busy taking care of my mother. When I actually have a free afternoon, I still want to see my friends and do things. I'm not avoiding anyone... I just don't have the free time I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or interest in activities you used to enjoy since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My appetite is pretty much the same as always. And I still enjoy doing things I've always liked... like reading or gardening... I just don't have as much time for them right now with my mother. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't felt sad or depressed. I mean, I get a little frustrated sometimes because I'm so tired from not sleeping well, but I wouldn't call it sadness. It's really just the constant worrying that's the issue for me. I don't feel down or hopeless or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel your worrying is causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's definitely affecting my work at the library. I've started missing these small but really important details... which is just not like me at all. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation to a colleague because I just... froze up. That really shook me, honestly. It's making me worry a lot about whether I can even keep leading my team properly if this keeps up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That is really not like me at all. It’s honestly made me worry a lot about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you to avoid making decisions or taking on new responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm actively avoiding new responsibilities, no. I mean, I still do my job and try to handle what comes my way. It's just that making decisions feels a lot harder now because I worry so much about making the wrong one. I haven't stepped down from anything or refused new tasks, it just... takes a lot more out of me to get through them.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant worrying?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for about a year and two months now. It all really started right after my mother had a fall and had to move into my home with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is worse than would be expected for the situations you're in?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really do. I mean, at first it made sense to be concerned, but over this past year, the worrying has just... spread. It went from just being about my mother’s safety to constantly worrying about finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and... and whether I’m being a good daughter. It's like even the really small things can start to feel like they’re about to completely unravel. So, yeah, it definitely feels like it's a lot more than what would be expected for the situations I'm actually in.\n\nDoctor: Would you consider your mother's fall and subsequent move into your home a traumatic event for you?\n\nPatient: I don't think I would call it traumatic, no. It was definitely stressful, and it changed my daily routine a lot, but... I wouldn't use the word trauma. It was just a really difficult adjustment that seemed to trigger all this stress.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying affected your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so, no. I mean, I haven't noticed any major issues. I probably see my friends a bit less these days just because my hands are full taking care of my mother, but... the worrying itself hasn't really caused problems or arguments with anyone. I mostly just keep all that stress to myself, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping due to your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, sleep has been pretty difficult lately. Most nights, I just... I lie there in bed, and my mind just keeps replaying everything that happened during the day. I end up trying to plan for every possible problem that could come up tomorrow. And then, if I happen to wake up in the middle of the night and hear my mother moving around in her room, that's it. I'm usually up for another hour or two just worrying. By the time I get to work at the library the next morning, I feel like I haven't really rested at all.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or activities with friends because of your worrying?\n\nPatient: No, not because of the worrying. Like I mentioned earlier, I am seeing people a bit less right now, but that's really just because I'm so busy taking care of my mother. When I actually have a free afternoon, I still want to see my friends and do things. I'm not avoiding anyone... I just don't have the free time I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or interest in activities you used to enjoy since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My appetite is pretty much the same as always. And I still enjoy doing things I've always liked... like reading or gardening... I just don't have as much time for them right now with my mother. But the interest is definitely still there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed since the worrying started?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't felt sad or depressed. I mean, I get a little frustrated sometimes because I'm so tired from not sleeping well, but I wouldn't call it sadness. It's really just the constant worrying that's the issue for me. I don't feel down or hopeless or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel your worrying is causing problems or making it hard for you to function?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's definitely affecting my work at the library. I've started missing these small but really important details... which is just not like me at all. And recently, I actually had to hand off parts of a grant presentation to a colleague because I just... froze up. That really shook me, honestly. It's making me worry a lot about whether I can even keep leading my team properly if this keeps up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy time with your family or perform daily tasks?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm still getting all my daily chores and errands done... the laundry, cooking, all of that is fine. I just might be worrying the whole time I'm doing it. And as for my family... no, I still enjoy being with them. Like I mentioned, I'm spending a lot of time helping my mother right now, and I still really value that time. \n\nSo at home, things are mostly functioning normally. It's really just at work where the worry seems to be causing actual problems for me."
